goods who can’t even do the job I spent my life getting good at.\n- key: S073\n  name: Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame\n  description: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences Yes… I do get startled pretty easily. I try to tell myself it’s normal, like anybody would jump at a loud noise, but it feels stronger than that sometimes.

the younger inspectors, and I’ve pulled back from community theater because I can’t reliably get through rehearsals.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 4 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since that machinery accident after the overnight schedule change, about 2 years and 4 months now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 59 / inspector_tester_sorter_sampler_or_weigher / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S077\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your anger or irritability often happens even when nothing particularly frustrating or upsetting is going on, or does it usually feel like there’s a clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I think… there’s usually some reason, at least in my head. I’m trying not to be that kind of person.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you’re easily startled by things—like loud noises or someone suddenly appearing—and does it feel like you’re always waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get startled pretty easily. It’s like I’m not trying to expect something bad, exactly… but my body is already waiting for it before I can talk myself out of it.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel fatigued or low on energy, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do get some sleep some nights, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it doesn’t feel very restful.\n\nMost nights I’m either lying there until it’s almost morning before I can fall asleep, or I jerk awake from dreams where I hear the line alarms and that awful metal sound. Then even if I’ve gotten a few hours, I wake up feeling like I never really slept. So yes, I guess the low energy is there a lot. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find that you're constantly scanning your environment for potential threats, even in places you know are safe, and does this feeling interfere with your ability to relax or focus on what you're doing?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I try not to make a big deal of it, because I know the break room or rehearsal aren’t dangerous places, but I’m still watching the doors, listening for sudden machine noises, and checking who’s moving behind me.

Is that… the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying a lot about things even when there's no immediate danger, or have trouble quieting your mind even when you want to relax?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do have trouble shutting it off, I guess. I don’t mean to sound dramatic, but even when nothing’s happening—like in the break room, or at rehearsal—I’m still watching the doors, listening for machine noises, checking if someone’s behind me. I know, logically, I’m probably fine, but my body just doesn’t seem convinced. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying about a variety of things — like work, relationships, or everyday responsibilities — even when there’s no clear reason to be concerned?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s a whole variety of things, no. Does that answer it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Can you recall specific situations where your anger or irritability felt out of proportion to what was happening, and did these outbursts happen more than once in a short period of time, like several times in one week?\n\nPatient: I… yes, I guess I can think of times. I don’t like saying “outbursts,” but there was a safety meeting where I started yelling before I could really stop myself. It felt like it came out bigger than the situation probably called for.\n\nAnd it hasn’t been just once. I keep snapping at people at work, enough that some of the younger inspectors seem nervous to come ask me things now. I feel bad about that. I’m not trying to scare anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless or on edge even in places where you feel safe, like at home, or when you’re alone?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I try not to make a big thing of it, because I know I’m probably safe. But even in the break room, or at rehearsal, I catch myself watching the doors and listening for sudden machine noises. I’m checking who’s moving behind me before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nIt’s like my head knows nothing’s happening, but my body doesn’t really believe it. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time recently when you felt genuinely happy or satisfied, even for a short while?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… I mean, I’m sure there have been little moments. Maybe when something at work goes smoothly, or I get home and can just sit for a minute. I don’t know if I’d call it genuinely happy exactly, but satisfied, maybe briefly.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s just… nothing big is coming to mind right away. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your anger or irritability often happens even when nothing particularly frustrating or upsetting is going on, or does it usually feel like there’s a clear reason for it?\n\nPatient: I think… there’s usually some reason, at least in my head. Like at work, if something feels unsafe or somebody isn’t listening, I can snap too fast. That safety meeting is the one I keep thinking about—I started yelling before I really had a chance to stop myself.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m just angry all the time for no reason. But it does happen enough that people notice. Some of the younger inspectors seem scared to come ask me things now, and I hate that. I’m trying not to be that kind of person.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you’re easily startled by things—like loud noises or someone suddenly appearing—and does it feel like you’re always waiting for something bad to happen?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do get startled pretty easily. I try to tell myself it’s normal, like anybody would jump at a loud noise, but it feels stronger than that sometimes.\n\nEven in the break room or at rehearsal, where I should know I’m okay, I’m still watching the doors, listening for sudden machine sounds, checking who’s moving behind me. It’s like I’m not trying to expect something bad, exactly… but my body is already waiting for it before I can talk myself out of it.\n\nI don’t know if that answers it right.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you feel fatigued or low on energy, even after getting enough rest?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I do get some sleep some nights, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it doesn’t feel very restful.\n\nMost nights I’m either lying there until it’s almost morning before I can fall asleep, or I jerk awake from dreams where I hear the line alarms and that awful metal sound. Then even if I’ve gotten a few hours, I wake up feeling like I never really slept. So yes, I guess the low energy is there a lot. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're constantly scanning your environment for potential threats, even in places you know are safe, and does this feeling interfere with your ability to relax or focus on what you're doing?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s a fair way to put it. I try not to make a big deal of it, because I know the break room or rehearsal aren’t dangerous places, but I’m still watching the doors, listening for sudden machine noises, and checking who’s moving behind me.\n\nIt’s like my head knows I’m okay, but my body doesn’t really believe it. And yes, it does make it harder to relax or stay focused. I can still do what I’m supposed to do, mostly, but part of me is always… somewhere else, keeping track. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, where you’re constantly on edge and scanning your environment, even in places you know are safe?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. Since the machinery accident, really—the one after they changed us to that overnight schedule.\n\nI kept thinking it would settle down once some time passed, and maybe I told myself it had to be getting better, but if I’m being honest, it’s been about two years and four months now. That sounds like a long time when I say it out loud. Is that the kind of timeframe you’re asking for?"
